========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's... I guess it's a combination of things, really. See, my father-in-law recently moved in with us, which, you know, we had to do it, it's family, but it completely upended the dynamic of the house. And the thing is, I'm an EMT, so I am absolutely no stranger to high-pressure situations. I mean, I see the worst of the worst out there on the streets, and I can handle that. I'm holding it together perfectly fine at work. I put on the uniform, I do my job, I'm dialed in. 

But the second I clock out and come home... or, well, even when I'm just trying to sit down in the evening and focus on my coursework—I'm taking some classes right now to finish up my Bachelor's degree—I just find myself incredibly wound up. Just tight in the chest, you know? I'm short with everyone, which really isn't my normal baseline. I'm usually a pretty even-keeled guy. 

And I think part of the problem, or maybe a result of the problem, is that I've completely stopped doing the things that usually keep my head clear. Like, I love fishing. I usually try to get out to the water at least a couple times a month, just to decompress and get away from the noise. And I have a whole woodworking setup in the garage that I haven't even looked at in weeks. I've even started skipping out on these community first-aid workshops I usually help run on my weekends off. I just... I don't have the bandwidth for any of it right now, and sitting around the house all wound up is just making it worse, so I figured I better come talk to somebody.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the distinction I guess I need to make. It's not that I don't think I'd enjoy them if I was actually doing them. I mean, I've been fishing since I was a little kid—my old man used to take me out to this little spot by the dam every Sunday, rain or shine—so that love for the water, it's baked into me. I know I'd still like it. And the woodworking, too. I've actually got this really beautiful piece of cherry wood just sitting out there on the workbench right now. I was in the middle of planning out a new coffee table for the living room, something with a nice live edge, you know? I had the whole design sketched out, the epoxy bought, the sander ready to go. 

But it's not a lack of enjoyment, it's more like... I don't know, a lack of activation energy? I just look at the door to the garage, and I think about going out there and setting up, and I just can't muster the drive to actually turn the doorknob. Because my brain is just buzzing so loud with everything else—the coursework deadlines, the constant shuffling around my father-in-law. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. 

So, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. I just haven't even tried to do them lately to find out. Getting started just feels like climbing a mountain right now, so I just end up sitting on the couch instead, feeling my chest get tighter.

Well, no, it's not that I don't care. I care a lot, actually. That's kind of what's so frustrating about this whole situation. I mean, I sit there on the couch, and I can literally see my tackle box sitting by the back door where I left it after my last trip, which was... gosh, it must have been early last month before my father-in-law's lease was up and we had to move him in. So the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I want to be doing those things. I miss doing those things. It's just... like I was saying before, it's the getting up and actually doing it part that feels impossible right now. It's like my battery is just completely drained by the time I have a free moment. I'll have a whole Sunday afternoon wide open, the weather will be perfect, sixty-five degrees and partly cloudy, ideal for getting out on the water, and I just... I can't. I just sit there feeling wound up and tense, wishing I was fishing, but not having the energy to actually put the gear in the truck and drive to the lake. So, yeah, I still care. Very much so. It just doesn't seem to translate into action right now.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not... not exactly. I wouldn't say I feel *down*, like, you know, sad or depressed or anything like that. It's not a sadness. It's more of this... this constant, low-level hum of tension, like I mentioned with the tight chest. It's like I'm a coiled spring, just waiting for the next thing to snap. 

And as for losing interest, well, I think we kind of covered that, right? I haven't lost interest. I still *want* to be out in the garage sanding that cherry wood, or out on the lake with my rod and reel. I really do. The interest is 100% there. It's just the execution that's failing me. 

So, no, I wouldn't say I've had days where I'm just sitting around feeling down and not caring about anything. It's almost the opposite. I care too much, I'm wound too tight, and my brain is just so overloaded with the coursework and the new living situation with my father-in-law that I just... I freeze up. I just sit there on the couch, tense as can be, thinking about all the things I *should* be doing, or *want* to be doing, and just not doing them. But it's definitely not a feeling of being down or sad. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. Overwhelmed and exhausted.

I was in the middle of planning out a new coffee table for the living room, something with a nice live edge, you know? I had the whole design sketched out, the epoxy bought, the sander ready to go. \n\nBut it's not a lack of enjoyment, it's more like... I don't know, a lack of activation energy? I just look at the door to the garage, and I think about going out there and setting up, and I just can't muster the drive to actually turn the doorknob. Because my brain is just buzzing so loud with everything else—the coursework deadlines, the constant shuffling around my father-in-law. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. I just haven't even tried to do them lately to find out. Getting started just feels like climbing a mountain right now, so I just end up sitting on the couch instead, feeling my chest get tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about doing things you used to enjoy, even when you have the chance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not that I don't care. I care a lot, actually. That's kind of what's so frustrating about this whole situation. I mean, I sit there on the couch, and I can literally see my tackle box sitting by the back door where I left it after my last trip, which was... gosh, it must have been early last month before my father-in-law's lease was up and we had to move him in. And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I want to be doing those things. I miss doing those things. It's just... like I was saying before, it's the getting up and actually doing it part that feels impossible right now. It's like my battery is just completely drained by the time I have a free moment. I'll have a whole Sunday afternoon wide open, the weather will be perfect, sixty-five degrees and partly cloudy, ideal for getting out on the water, and I just... I can't. I just sit there feeling wound up and tense, wishing I was fishing, but not having the energy to actually put the gear in the truck and drive to the lake. So, yeah, I still care. Very much so. It just doesn't seem to translate into action right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly. I wouldn't say I feel *down*, like, you know, sad or depressed or anything like that. It's not a sadness. It's more of this... this constant, low-level hum of tension, like I mentioned with the tight chest. It's like I'm a coiled spring, just waiting for the next thing to snap. \n\nAnd as for losing interest, well, I think we kind of covered that, right? I haven't lost interest. I still *want* to be out in the garage sanding that cherry wood, or out on the lake with my rod and reel. I really do. The interest is 100% there. It's just the execution that's failing me. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've had days where I'm just sitting around feeling down and not caring about anything. It's almost the opposite. I care too much, I'm wound too tight, and my brain is just so overloaded with the coursework and the new living situation with my father-in-law that I just... I freeze up. I just sit there on the couch, tense as can be, thinking about all the things I *should* be doing, or *want* to be doing, and just not doing them. But it's definitely not a feeling of being down or sad. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. Overwhelmed and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting more irritable or angry lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess that’s exactly it. That’s really the whole reason I made the appointment to come in. It’s like, at work—and being an EMT, you know work is high stakes, it’s literally life or death sometimes—I can keep it together. I’m focused, I’m professional, I’m doing my job. But the second I clock out and come home... it’s a different story. \n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve just been so wound up. Like I said, he’s got the TV blasting, he’s always shuffling around, and I’m just... I’m definitely shorter with people than I’m used to being. It doesn't take much to set me off. And it’s bleeding into my coursework, too. I just sit there staring at the screen, feeling my jaw clench, getting frustrated over the smallest little assignment. \n\nAnd because I’m so on edge, I’ve started skipping all the things that usually keep my head clear. I used to love doing those, sharing some of my field experience, you know? But now, I just can't. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. 

It's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.

I know I'd still like it. And the woodworking, too. I've actually got this really beautiful piece of cherry wood just sitting out there on the workbench right now. I was in the middle of planning out a new coffee table for the living room, something with a nice live edge, you know? I had the whole design sketched out, the epoxy bought, the sander ready to go. \n\nBut it's not a lack of enjoyment, it's more like... I don't know, a lack of activation energy? I just look at the door to the garage, and I think about going out there and setting up, and I just can't muster the drive to actually turn the doorknob. Because my brain is just buzzing so loud with everything else—the coursework deadlines, the constant shuffling around my father-in-law. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. I just haven't even tried to do them lately to find out. Getting started just feels like climbing a mountain right now, so I just end up sitting on the couch instead, feeling my chest get tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about doing things you used to enjoy, even when you have the chance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not that I don't care. I care a lot, actually. That's kind of what's so frustrating about this whole situation. I mean, I sit there on the couch, and I can literally see my tackle box sitting by the back door where I left it after my last trip, which was... gosh, it must have been early last month before my father-in-law's lease was up and we had to move him in. And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I want to be doing those things. I miss doing those things. It's just... like I was saying before, it's the getting up and actually doing it part that feels impossible right now. I'll have a whole Sunday afternoon wide open, the weather will be perfect, sixty-five degrees and partly cloudy, ideal for getting out on the water, and I just... I can't. I still *want* to be out in the garage sanding that cherry wood, or out on the lake with my rod and reel. I really do. The interest is 100% there. It's just the execution that's failing me. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've had days where I'm just sitting around feeling down and not caring about anything. It's almost the opposite. I care too much, I'm wound too tight, and my brain is just so overloaded with the coursework and the new living situation with my father-in-law that I just... I freeze up. I just sit there on the couch, tense as can be, thinking about all the things I *should* be doing, or *want* to be doing, and just not doing them. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. 

But as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. 

It's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.

Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. 

It's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? Like, I'll snap at my wife because the TV is too loud, and then I'll feel guilty about that, because it's not her fault her dad needs to live with us right now. But it's not this deep, dark feeling of worthlessness. It's just... frustration. Frustration that I can't seem to just put on a pair of noise-canceling headphones and power through my reading like a normal person. 

But I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person or a failure. I just think I'm a guy who is incredibly stressed out by a major change in his living situation and hasn't figured out how to adapt to it yet. I mean, anyone would be stressed if their quiet sanctuary suddenly turned into a 24/7 game show broadcasting network, right? So, no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling worse about myself in that way. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.

I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. \n\nIt's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? Like, I'll snap at my wife because the TV is too loud, and then I'll feel guilty about that, because it's not her fault her dad needs to live with us right now. But it's not this deep, dark feeling of worthlessness. It's just... frustration. Frustration that I can't seem to just put on a pair of noise-canceling headphones and power through my reading like a normal person. \n\nBut I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person or a failure. I just think I'm a guy who is incredibly stressed out by a major change in his living situation and hasn't figured out how to adapt to it yet. I mean, anyone would be stressed if their quiet sanctuary suddenly turned into a 24/7 game show broadcasting network, right? So, no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling worse about myself in that way. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even when you have the chance to do them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 51 / emergency_medical_technician / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not a loss of interest. I still *want* to do the things I enjoy. Like, I was saying earlier about my garage—I have this whole setup out there, a workbench where I do some woodworking, restore old furniture, that kind of thing. It's usually my way to decompress after a rough shift. And I still really, really want to be out there. I'll be sitting on the couch, hearing the TV blaring, and I'll be thinking about this oak coffee table I started sanding down three months ago, right before he moved in. I can picture exactly what I need to do next, which grit of sandpaper to use, how I want the stain to look... 

So the interest is absolutely still there. It's not like I look at my tools and feel nothing, or like I don't care about my hobbies anymore. It's just that I can't seem to get myself out there to actually *do* it. Because by the time I have a free hour, I'm just so mentally drained from the noise and the tension in the house that I just sit there. It's like I'm paralyzed by the environment. 

And it's the same with my coursework. I'm actually really interested in the material, it's for an advanced certification I've wanted to get for years. I *want* to read the textbook, I *want* to write the papers. But I just can't focus. So, if I had the chance—like, if the house was completely empty and quiet for a whole weekend—I know for a fact I'd be out in the garage in a heartbeat, or happily buried in my books. The desire hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... the execution is blocked by all this stress and noise. So no, I haven't lost interest or pleasure in those things. I just haven't been able to actually engage with them lately.

And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I care too much, I'm wound too tight, and my brain is just so overloaded with the coursework and the new living situation with my father-in-law that I just... I freeze up. I’m focused, I’m professional, I’m doing my job. But the second I clock out and come home... it’s a different story. \n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve just been so wound up. Like I said, he’s got the TV blasting, he’s always shuffling around, and I’m just... I’m definitely shorter with people than I’m used to being. It doesn't take much to set me off. And it’s bleeding into my coursework, too. I just sit there staring at the screen, feeling my jaw clench, getting frustrated over the smallest little assignment. \n\nAnd because I’m so on edge, I’ve started skipping all the things that usually keep my head clear. I’m not fishing, I’m not touching the woodworking projects, I even stopped going to these first-aid workshops I usually help run on the weekends. I used to love doing those, sharing some of my field experience, you know? But now, I just can't. I’m just so tightly wound all the time at home, snapping at little things, that I just shut down and avoid everything that usually helps me decompress.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt unusually tired or noticed a big drop in your energy levels, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. \n\nIt's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? I just think I'm a guy who is incredibly stressed out by a major change in his living situation and hasn't figured out how to adapt to it yet. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even when you have the chance to do them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a loss of interest. I still *want* to do the things I enjoy. It's usually my way to decompress after a rough shift. And I still really, really want to be out there. I'll be sitting on the couch, hearing the TV blaring, and I'll be thinking about this oak coffee table I started sanding down three months ago, right before he moved in. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really worrying. I mean, I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around chewing my fingernails, thinking, "Oh no, what if this happens, what if that happens?" Like, with work, I don't worry about work when I'm not there. I've been doing this for over twenty years. When I clock out, I clock out. I don't lay in bed stressing about whether I missed a vein on an IV or if I should have done something differently on a call. I leave it at the station. 

And with school... I don't worry that I'm going to fail, like I said before. I know I can do the work. The only thing I guess you could say I "worry" about is just the logistics of it all. Like, how am I going to find three quiet hours to get this paper done before Sunday at midnight? But that's not, you know, irrational worrying. That's just a very real, practical problem caused by the fact that my living room has turned into a retirement home common area. 

So it's not like my mind is spinning with all these hypothetical disasters. I'm not anxious about the future or anything like that. I'm just incredibly focused on the immediate problem, which is the noise and the disruption in my house. I mean, my father-in-law, he's a good guy, he really is, and I know it's hard for him right now, losing his independence and having to move in with his daughter and son-in-law. I get that. I try to be empathetic. But my God, the volume on that television. It's like a physical force. It rattles my teeth. And it's constant. From the minute he wakes up at 6 AM to the minute he finally falls asleep in his recliner at 10 PM. 

So my brain is just entirely occupied by that. It's not worrying, it's just... hyper-awareness of the annoyance. I'll be at work, and instead of worrying about my next call, I'll just be dreading walking through my front door because I know exactly what it's going to sound like. But I don't sit around inventing things to be anxious about. The actual reality of my day-to-day life is stressful enough without making things up to worry about, you know?

It's not that I don't think I'd enjoy them if I was actually doing them. I mean, I've been fishing since I was a little kid—my old man used to take me out to this little spot by the dam every Sunday, rain or shine—so that love for the water, it's baked into me. I know I'd still like it. And the woodworking, too. I've actually got this really beautiful piece of cherry wood just sitting out there on the workbench right now. I was in the middle of planning out a new coffee table for the living room, something with a nice live edge, you know? I had the whole design sketched out, the epoxy bought, the sander ready to go. \n\nBut it's not a lack of enjoyment, it's more like... I don't know, a lack of activation energy? I just look at the door to the garage, and I think about going out there and setting up, and I just can't muster the drive to actually turn the doorknob. Because my brain is just buzzing so loud with everything else—the coursework deadlines, the constant shuffling around my father-in-law. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. I just haven't even tried to do them lately to find out. Getting started just feels like climbing a mountain right now, so I just end up sitting on the couch instead, feeling my chest get tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about doing things you used to enjoy, even when you have the chance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not that I don't care. I care a lot, actually. That's kind of what's so frustrating about this whole situation. I mean, I sit there on the couch, and I can literally see my tackle box sitting by the back door where I left it after my last trip, which was... gosh, it must have been early last month before my father-in-law's lease was up and we had to move him in. And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I want to be doing those things. I miss doing those things. It's just... like I was saying before, it's the getting up and actually doing it part that feels impossible right now. It's like my battery is just completely drained by the time I have a free moment. I'll have a whole Sunday afternoon wide open, the weather will be perfect, sixty-five degrees and partly cloudy, ideal for getting out on the water, and I just... I can't. I just sit there feeling wound up and tense, wishing I was fishing, but not having the energy to actually put the gear in the truck and drive to the lake. So, yeah, I still care. Very much so. It just doesn't seem to translate into action right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly. I wouldn't say I feel *down*, like, you know, sad or depressed or anything like that. It's not a sadness. It's more of this... this constant, low-level hum of tension, like I mentioned with the tight chest. It's like I'm a coiled spring, just waiting for the next thing to snap. \n\nAnd as for losing interest, well, I think we kind of covered that, right? I haven't lost interest. I still *want* to be out in the garage sanding that cherry wood, or out on the lake with my rod and reel. I really do. The interest is 100% there. It's just the execution that's failing me. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've had days where I'm just sitting around feeling down and not caring about anything. It's almost the opposite. I care too much, I'm wound too tight, and my brain is just so overloaded with the coursework and the new living situation with my father-in-law that I just... I freeze up. I just sit there on the couch, tense as can be, thinking about all the things I *should* be doing, or *want* to be doing, and just not doing them. But it's definitely not a feeling of being down or sad. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. Overwhelmed and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting more irritable or angry lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess that’s exactly it. That’s really the whole reason I made the appointment to come in. It’s like, at work—and being an EMT, you know work is high stakes, it’s literally life or death sometimes—I can keep it together. I’m focused, I’m professional, I’m doing my job. But the second I clock out and come home... it’s a different story. \n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve just been so wound up. Like I said, he’s got the TV blasting, he’s always shuffling around, and I’m just... I’m definitely shorter with people than I’m used to being. It doesn't take much to set me off. And it’s bleeding into my coursework, too. I just sit there staring at the screen, feeling my jaw clench, getting frustrated over the smallest little assignment. \n\nAnd because I’m so on edge, I’ve started skipping all the things that usually keep my head clear. I’m not fishing, I’m not touching the woodworking projects, I even stopped going to these first-aid workshops I usually help run on the weekends. I used to love doing those, sharing some of my field experience, you know? But now, I just can't. I’m just so tightly wound all the time at home, snapping at little things, that I just shut down and avoid everything that usually helps me decompress.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt unusually tired or noticed a big drop in your energy levels, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. \n\nIt's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? Like, I'll snap at my wife because the TV is too loud, and then I'll feel guilty about that, because it's not her fault her dad needs to live with us right now. But it's not this deep, dark feeling of worthlessness. It's just... frustration. Frustration that I can't seem to just put on a pair of noise-canceling headphones and power through my reading like a normal person. \n\nBut I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person or a failure. I just think I'm a guy who is incredibly stressed out by a major change in his living situation and hasn't figured out how to adapt to it yet. I mean, anyone would be stressed if their quiet sanctuary suddenly turned into a 24/7 game show broadcasting network, right? So, no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling worse about myself in that way. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even when you have the chance to do them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a loss of interest. I still *want* to do the things I enjoy. Like, I was saying earlier about my garage—I have this whole setup out there, a workbench where I do some woodworking, restore old furniture, that kind of thing. It's usually my way to decompress after a rough shift. And I still really, really want to be out there. I'll be sitting on the couch, hearing the TV blaring, and I'll be thinking about this oak coffee table I started sanding down three months ago, right before he moved in. I can picture exactly what I need to do next, which grit of sandpaper to use, how I want the stain to look... \n\nSo the interest is absolutely still there. It's not like I look at my tools and feel nothing, or like I don't care about my hobbies anymore. It's just that I can't seem to get myself out there to actually *do* it. Because by the time I have a free hour, I'm just so mentally drained from the noise and the tension in the house that I just sit there. It's like I'm paralyzed by the environment. \n\nAnd it's the same with my coursework. I'm actually really interested in the material, it's for an advanced certification I've wanted to get for years. I *want* to read the textbook, I *want* to write the papers. But I just can't focus. So, if I had the chance—like, if the house was completely empty and quiet for a whole weekend—I know for a fact I'd be out in the garage in a heartbeat, or happily buried in my books. The desire hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... the execution is blocked by all this stress and noise. So no, I haven't lost interest or pleasure in those things. I just haven't been able to actually engage with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself worrying a lot about different things—like work, school, or home life—even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. I mean, I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around chewing my fingernails, thinking, \"Oh no, what if this happens, what if that happens?\" Like, with work, I don't worry about work when I'm not there. I've been doing this for over twenty years. When I clock out, I clock out. I don't lay in bed stressing about whether I missed a vein on an IV or if I should have done something differently on a call. I leave it at the station. \n\nAnd with school... I don't worry that I'm going to fail, like I said before. I know I can do the work. The only thing I guess you could say I \"worry\" about is just the logistics of it all. Like, how am I going to find three quiet hours to get this paper done before Sunday at midnight? But that's not, you know, irrational worrying. That's just a very real, practical problem caused by the fact that my living room has turned into a retirement home common area. \n\nSo it's not like my mind is spinning with all these hypothetical disasters. I'm not anxious about the future or anything like that. I'm just incredibly focused on the immediate problem, which is the noise and the disruption in my house. I mean, my father-in-law, he's a good guy, he really is, and I know it's hard for him right now, losing his independence and having to move in with his daughter and son-in-law. I get that. I try to be empathetic. But my God, the volume on that television. It's like a physical force. It rattles my teeth. And it's constant. From the minute he wakes up at 6 AM to the minute he finally falls asleep in his recliner at 10 PM. \n\nSo my brain is just entirely occupied by that. It's not worrying, it's just... hyper-awareness of the annoyance. I'll be at work, and instead of worrying about my next call, I'll just be dreading walking through my front door because I know exactly what it's going to sound like. But I don't sit around inventing things to be anxious about. The actual reality of my day-to-day life is stressful enough without making things up to worry about, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, my sleep is actually... it's probably the one thing that hasn't been completely derailed by all of this. I mean, you would think, with all the noise and the frustration I was just telling you about, that I'd be tossing and turning all night, but honestly? Once I actually get into bed, I am out cold. 

I think a lot of that just comes down to my job. When you've been riding in an ambulance for over twenty years, you learn to sleep when you can, where you can. You train your body to just shut down the second your head hits a pillow, because you never know if you're going to get a call in ten minutes and be up for the next fourteen hours straight on a messy trauma. So I've got this, I don't know, this built-in ability to just crash from pure physical exhaustion. 

And, to be fair, our master bedroom is at the very back of the house. It's down the hall, past the kitchen, so the TV noise isn't quite as bad back there. Plus, like I mentioned, my father-in-law usually falls asleep in his recliner around 10 PM anyway. He'll just leave the TV blaring, but my wife will usually creep out to the living room and mute it or turn it way down once she hears him snoring. And she bought this white noise machine a couple of years ago—I used to absolutely hate that thing, I thought it sounded like a broken radio, but now? Now it is an absolute lifesaver. It just washes out whatever residual noise is coming down the hallway. 

So, no, falling asleep isn't an issue for me at all. I sleep right through the night. And I'm not staying in bed all day or oversleeping, either. I still get up at my usual time, usually around 5:30 or 6:00 in the morning. I get up, I make my coffee, and I try to enjoy exactly thirty or forty minutes of absolute silence before I hear the click of the remote control in the living room and the morning news starts blasting at full volume. So my sleep schedule is exactly the same as it's always been. It's really just the hours I'm awake that are the problem.

I'm holding it together perfectly fine at work. I put on the uniform, I do my job, I'm dialed in. \n\nBut the second I clock out and come home... or, well, even when I'm just trying to sit down in the evening and focus on my coursework—I'm taking some classes right now to finish up my Bachelor's degree—I just find myself incredibly wound up. Like, I love fishing. I usually try to get out to the water at least a couple times a month, just to decompress and get away from the noise. And I have a whole woodworking setup in the garage that I haven't even looked at in weeks. I've even started skipping out on these community first-aid workshops I usually help run on my weekends off. I just... I don't have the bandwidth for any of it right now, and sitting around the house all wound up is just making it worse, so I figured I better come talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting the same enjoyment out of things you used to really like, like fishing or woodworking?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the distinction I guess I need to make. I mean, I've been fishing since I was a little kid—my old man used to take me out to this little spot by the dam every Sunday, rain or shine—so that love for the water, it's baked into me. I know I'd still like it. And the woodworking, too. I've actually got this really beautiful piece of cherry wood just sitting out there on the workbench right now. I just look at the door to the garage, and I think about going out there and setting up, and I just can't muster the drive to actually turn the doorknob. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. I just haven't even tried to do them lately to find out. Getting started just feels like climbing a mountain right now, so I just end up sitting on the couch instead, feeling my chest get tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about doing things you used to enjoy, even when you have the chance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not that I don't care. I care a lot, actually. That's kind of what's so frustrating about this whole situation. I mean, I sit there on the couch, and I can literally see my tackle box sitting by the back door where I left it after my last trip, which was... gosh, it must have been early last month before my father-in-law's lease was up and we had to move him in. And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I want to be doing those things. I miss doing those things. It's just... like I was saying before, it's the getting up and actually doing it part that feels impossible right now. I'll have a whole Sunday afternoon wide open, the weather will be perfect, sixty-five degrees and partly cloudy, ideal for getting out on the water, and I just... I can't. I just sit there feeling wound up and tense, wishing I was fishing, but not having the energy to actually put the gear in the truck and drive to the lake. So, yeah, I still care. Very much so. It just doesn't seem to translate into action right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly. It's more of this... this constant, low-level hum of tension, like I mentioned with the tight chest. It's like I'm a coiled spring, just waiting for the next thing to snap. \n\nAnd as for losing interest, well, I think we kind of covered that, right? I haven't lost interest. I still *want* to be out in the garage sanding that cherry wood, or out on the lake with my rod and reel. I really do. The interest is 100% there. It's just the execution that's failing me. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've had days where I'm just sitting around feeling down and not caring about anything. It's almost the opposite. I care too much, I'm wound too tight, and my brain is just so overloaded with the coursework and the new living situation with my father-in-law that I just... I freeze up. I just sit there on the couch, tense as can be, thinking about all the things I *should* be doing, or *want* to be doing, and just not doing them. But it's definitely not a feeling of being down or sad. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. Overwhelmed and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting more irritable or angry lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess that’s exactly it. That’s really the whole reason I made the appointment to come in. It’s like, at work—and being an EMT, you know work is high stakes, it’s literally life or death sometimes—I can keep it together. I’m focused, I’m professional, I’m doing my job. But the second I clock out and come home... it’s a different story. \n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve just been so wound up. And it’s bleeding into my coursework, too. I just sit there staring at the screen, feeling my jaw clench, getting frustrated over the smallest little assignment. \n\nAnd because I’m so on edge, I’ve started skipping all the things that usually keep my head clear. I’m not fishing, I’m not touching the woodworking projects, I even stopped going to these first-aid workshops I usually help run on the weekends. I used to love doing those, sharing some of my field experience, you know? But now, I just can't. I’m just so tightly wound all the time at home, snapping at little things, that I just shut down and avoid everything that usually helps me decompress.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt unusually tired or noticed a big drop in your energy levels, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. \n\nIt's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? Like, I'll snap at my wife because the TV is too loud, and then I'll feel guilty about that, because it's not her fault her dad needs to live with us right now. But it's not this deep, dark feeling of worthlessness. It's just... frustration. Frustration that I can't seem to just put on a pair of noise-canceling headphones and power through my reading like a normal person. \n\nBut I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person or a failure. I just think I'm a guy who is incredibly stressed out by a major change in his living situation and hasn't figured out how to adapt to it yet. I mean, anyone would be stressed if their quiet sanctuary suddenly turned into a 24/7 game show broadcasting network, right? So, no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling worse about myself in that way. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even when you have the chance to do them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a loss of interest. I still *want* to do the things I enjoy. Like, I was saying earlier about my garage—I have this whole setup out there, a workbench where I do some woodworking, restore old furniture, that kind of thing. It's usually my way to decompress after a rough shift. And I still really, really want to be out there. I'll be sitting on the couch, hearing the TV blaring, and I'll be thinking about this oak coffee table I started sanding down three months ago, right before he moved in. I can picture exactly what I need to do next, which grit of sandpaper to use, how I want the stain to look... \n\nSo the interest is absolutely still there. It's not like I look at my tools and feel nothing, or like I don't care about my hobbies anymore. It's just that I can't seem to get myself out there to actually *do* it. Because by the time I have a free hour, I'm just so mentally drained from the noise and the tension in the house that I just sit there. It's like I'm paralyzed by the environment. \n\nAnd it's the same with my coursework. I'm actually really interested in the material, it's for an advanced certification I've wanted to get for years. I *want* to read the textbook, I *want* to write the papers. But I just can't focus. So, if I had the chance—like, if the house was completely empty and quiet for a whole weekend—I know for a fact I'd be out in the garage in a heartbeat, or happily buried in my books. The desire hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... the execution is blocked by all this stress and noise. So no, I haven't lost interest or pleasure in those things. I just haven't been able to actually engage with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself worrying a lot about different things—like work, school, or home life—even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. I mean, I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around chewing my fingernails, thinking, \"Oh no, what if this happens, what if that happens?\" Like, with work, I don't worry about work when I'm not there. I've been doing this for over twenty years. When I clock out, I clock out. I don't lay in bed stressing about whether I missed a vein on an IV or if I should have done something differently on a call. I leave it at the station. \n\nAnd with school... I don't worry that I'm going to fail, like I said before. I know I can do the work. The only thing I guess you could say I \"worry\" about is just the logistics of it all. Like, how am I going to find three quiet hours to get this paper done before Sunday at midnight? But that's not, you know, irrational worrying. That's just a very real, practical problem caused by the fact that my living room has turned into a retirement home common area. \n\nSo it's not like my mind is spinning with all these hypothetical disasters. I'm not anxious about the future or anything like that. I'm just incredibly focused on the immediate problem, which is the noise and the disruption in my house. I mean, my father-in-law, he's a good guy, he really is, and I know it's hard for him right now, losing his independence and having to move in with his daughter and son-in-law. I get that. I try to be empathetic. But my God, the volume on that television. It's like a physical force. It rattles my teeth. And it's constant. From the minute he wakes up at 6 AM to the minute he finally falls asleep in his recliner at 10 PM. \n\nSo my brain is just entirely occupied by that. It's not worrying, it's just... hyper-awareness of the annoyance. I'll be at work, and instead of worrying about my next call, I'll just be dreading walking through my front door because I know exactly what it's going to sound like. But I don't sit around inventing things to be anxious about. The actual reality of my day-to-day life is stressful enough without making things up to worry about, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your sleep lately, like having trouble falling or staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, my sleep is actually... it's probably the one thing that hasn't been completely derailed by all of this. I mean, you would think, with all the noise and the frustration I was just telling you about, that I'd be tossing and turning all night, but honestly? Once I actually get into bed, I am out cold. \n\nI think a lot of that just comes down to my job. When you've been riding in an ambulance for over twenty years, you learn to sleep when you can, where you can. You train your body to just shut down the second your head hits a pillow, because you never know if you're going to get a call in ten minutes and be up for the next fourteen hours straight on a messy trauma. So I've got this, I don't know, this built-in ability to just crash from pure physical exhaustion. \n\nAnd, to be fair, our master bedroom is at the very back of the house. It's down the hall, past the kitchen, so the TV noise isn't quite as bad back there. Plus, like I mentioned, my father-in-law usually falls asleep in his recliner around 10 PM anyway. He'll just leave the TV blaring, but my wife will usually creep out to the living room and mute it or turn it way down once she hears him snoring. And she bought this white noise machine a couple of years ago—I used to absolutely hate that thing, I thought it sounded like a broken radio, but now? Now it is an absolute lifesaver. It just washes out whatever residual noise is coming down the hallway. \n\nSo, no, falling asleep isn't an issue for me at all. I sleep right through the night. And I'm not staying in bed all day or oversleeping, either. I still get up at my usual time, usually around 5:30 or 6:00 in the morning. I get up, I make my coffee, and I try to enjoy exactly thirty or forty minutes of absolute silence before I hear the click of the remote control in the living room and the morning news starts blasting at full volume. So my sleep schedule is exactly the same as it's always been. It's really just the hours I'm awake that are the problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No new disorders are indicated at this time.", "is_final": false}

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that's exactly what happens. It's incredibly frustrating. Because, you know, my actual job—the EMT work—I’m still doing that just fine. When I'm on shift, I'm 100% there. Twenty years in the back of an ambulance, muscle memory just takes over. I show up, I do the job, I don't miss shifts. Work is almost like an escape at this point. 

But everything else? It's exactly like you said. Even when I do get a window of time, I just stall out. Like that paramedic coursework I was telling you about earlier. I've completely fallen behind on it. It's ridiculous because I really do want the certification, and I know the material. But say my wife takes her dad to a doctor's appointment, right? The house is finally dead silent for maybe two hours. I'll sit down at the kitchen table, I'll open the laptop, pull up the modules... and I just stare at the screen. I'll read the same paragraph about cardiac pharmacology four times and retain absolutely none of it. I just can't get the engine to turn over. I end up just scrolling on my phone or staring out the window until they get back and the TV goes right back on. 

And it's not just the schoolwork, either. It's bleeding into other commitments. There are these community first-aid workshops I usually help out with at the rec center a few blocks over. We teach CPR, basic wound care, how to use an AED, that sort of thing. I've been doing it for, gosh, maybe five or six years now. Usually it's just once a month on a Saturday morning, and I used to really look forward to it. It's good for the neighborhood, it gets me out of the house. But lately? I've stopped showing up consistently. I'll text the coordinator the night before and make up an excuse, say I'm too tired from a shift, or I'll just conveniently "forget" to put it on my calendar so I double-book myself. 

I feel terribly guilty about it, I really do, because they rely on me to bring the training dummies and run the demonstrations. But the thought of going over there and being "on" and trying to teach a room full of twenty people when my own brain feels like it's wading through molasses... I just can't bring myself to do it. So yeah, I'm still clocking in for my actual paycheck, but all the other stuff I'm supposed to be doing is just slipping right through the cracks, even when I technically have the time to do it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I guess it's a combination of things, really. I'm holding it together perfectly fine at work. I just... I mean, I've been fishing since I was a little kid—my old man used to take me out to this little spot by the dam every Sunday, rain or shine—so that love for the water, it's baked into me. I know I'd still like it. And the woodworking, too. I've actually got this really beautiful piece of cherry wood just sitting out there on the workbench right now. I was in the middle of planning out a new coffee table for the living room, something with a nice live edge, you know? I had the whole design sketched out, the epoxy bought, the sander ready to go. \n\nBut it's not a lack of enjoyment, it's more like... I don't know, a lack of activation energy? I just look at the door to the garage, and I think about going out there and setting up, and I just can't muster the drive to actually turn the doorknob. Because my brain is just buzzing so loud with everything else—the coursework deadlines, the constant shuffling around my father-in-law. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. I just haven't even tried to do them lately to find out. Getting started just feels like climbing a mountain right now, so I just end up sitting on the couch instead, feeling my chest get tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about doing things you used to enjoy, even when you have the chance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not that I don't care. I care a lot, actually. That's kind of what's so frustrating about this whole situation. I mean, I sit there on the couch, and I can literally see my tackle box sitting by the back door where I left it after my last trip, which was... gosh, it must have been early last month before my father-in-law's lease was up and we had to move him in. And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I want to be doing those things. I miss doing those things. It's just... like I was saying before, it's the getting up and actually doing it part that feels impossible right now. I'll have a whole Sunday afternoon wide open, the weather will be perfect, sixty-five degrees and partly cloudy, ideal for getting out on the water, and I just... I can't. It just doesn't seem to translate into action right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly. It's more of this... this constant, low-level hum of tension, like I mentioned with the tight chest. It's like I'm a coiled spring, just waiting for the next thing to snap. \n\nAnd as for losing interest, well, I think we kind of covered that, right? I haven't lost interest. I still *want* to be out in the garage sanding that cherry wood, or out on the lake with my rod and reel. I really do. The interest is 100% there. It's just the execution that's failing me. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've had days where I'm just sitting around feeling down and not caring about anything. It's almost the opposite. I just sit there on the couch, tense as can be, thinking about all the things I *should* be doing, or *want* to be doing, and just not doing them. But it's definitely not a feeling of being down or sad. Just... overwhelmed, I guess.
