That’s really the whole reason I made the appointment to come in. It’s like, at work—and being an EMT, you know work is high stakes, it’s literally life or death sometimes—I can keep it together. I’m focused, I’m professional, I’m doing my job. But the second I clock out and come home... it’s a different story. \n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve just been so wound up. Like I said, he’s got the TV blasting, he’s always shuffling around, and I’m just... I’m definitely shorter with people than I’m used to being. It doesn't take much to set me off. And it’s bleeding into my coursework, too. I just sit there staring at the screen, feeling my jaw clench, getting frustrated over the smallest little assignment. \n\nAnd because I’m so on edge, I’ve started skipping all the things that usually keep my head clear. I’m not fishing, I’m not touching the woodworking projects, I even stopped going to these first-aid workshops I usually help run on the weekends. I used to love doing those, sharing some of my field experience, you know? But now, I just can't. I’m just so tightly wound all the time at home, snapping at little things, that I just shut down and avoid everything that usually helps me decompress.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt unusually tired or noticed a big drop in your energy levels, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. \n\nIt's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? Like, I'll snap at my wife because the TV is too loud, and then I'll feel guilty about that, because it's not her fault her dad needs to live with us right now. But it's not this deep, dark feeling of worthlessness. It's just... frustration. Frustration that I can't seem to just put on a pair of noise-canceling headphones and power through my reading like a normal person. \n\nBut I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person or a failure. I just think I'm a guy who is incredibly stressed out by a major change in his living situation and hasn't figured out how to adapt to it yet. I mean, anyone would be stressed if their quiet sanctuary suddenly turned into a 24/7 game show broadcasting network, right? So, no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling worse about myself in that way. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even when you have the chance to do them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a loss of interest. I still *want* to do the things I enjoy. Like, I was saying earlier about my garage—I have this whole setup out there, a workbench where I do some woodworking, restore old furniture, that kind of thing. It's usually my way to decompress after a rough shift. And I still really, really want to be out there. I'll be sitting on the couch, hearing the TV blaring, and I'll be thinking about this oak coffee table I started sanding down three months ago, right before he moved in. I can picture exactly what I need to do next, which grit of sandpaper to use, how I want the stain to look... \n\nSo the interest is absolutely still there. It's not like I look at my tools and feel nothing, or like I don't care about my hobbies anymore. It's just that I can't seem to get myself out there to actually *do* it. Because by the time I have a free hour, I'm just so mentally drained from the noise and the tension in the house that I just sit there. It's like I'm paralyzed by the environment. \n\nAnd it's the same with my coursework. I'm actually really interested in the material, it's for an advanced certification I've wanted to get for years. I *want* to read the textbook, I *want* to write the papers. But I just can't focus. So, if I had the chance—like, if the house was completely empty and quiet for a whole weekend—I know for a fact I'd be out in the garage in a heartbeat, or happily buried in my books. The desire hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... the execution is blocked by all this stress and noise. So no, I haven't lost interest or pleasure in those things. I just haven't been able to actually engage with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself worrying a lot about different things—like work, school, or home life—even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. I mean, I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around chewing my fingernails, thinking, \"Oh no, what if this happens, what if that happens?\" Like, with work, I don't worry about work when I'm not there. I've been doing this for over twenty years. When I clock out, I clock out. I don't lay in bed stressing about whether I missed a vein on an IV or if I should have done something differently on a call. I leave it at the station. \n\nAnd with school... I don't worry that I'm going to fail, like I said before. I know I can do the work. The only thing I guess you could say I \"worry\" about is just the logistics of it all. Like, how am I going to find three quiet hours to get this paper done before Sunday at midnight? But that's not, you know, irrational worrying. That's just a very real, practical problem caused by the fact that my living room has turned into a retirement home common area. \n\nSo it's not like my mind is spinning with all these hypothetical disasters. I'm not anxious about the future or anything like that. I'm just incredibly focused on the immediate problem, which is the noise and the disruption in my house. I mean, my father-in-law, he's a good guy, he really is, and I know it's hard for him right now, losing his independence and having to move in with his daughter and son-in-law. I get that. I try to be empathetic. But my God, the volume on that television. It's like a physical force. It rattles my teeth. And it's constant. From the minute he wakes up at 6 AM to the minute he finally falls asleep in his recliner at 10 PM. \n\nSo my brain is just entirely occupied by that. It's not worrying, it's just... hyper-awareness of the annoyance. I'll be at work, and instead of worrying about my next call, I'll just be dreading walking through my front door because I know exactly what it's going to sound like. But I don't sit around inventing things to be anxious about. The actual reality of my day-to-day life is stressful enough without making things up to worry about, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your sleep lately, like having trouble falling or staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, my sleep is actually... it's probably the one thing that hasn't been completely derailed by all of this. I mean, you would think, with all the noise and the frustration I was just telling you about, that I'd be tossing and turning all night, but honestly? Once I actually get into bed, I am out cold. \n\nI think a lot of that just comes down to my job. When you've been riding in an ambulance for over twenty years, you learn to sleep when you can, where you can. You train your body to just shut down the second your head hits a pillow, because you never know if you're going to get a call in ten minutes and be up for the next fourteen hours straight on a messy trauma. So I've got this, I don't know, this built-in ability to just crash from pure physical exhaustion. \n\nAnd, to be fair, our master bedroom is at the very back of the house. It's down the hall, past the kitchen, so the TV noise isn't quite as bad back there. Plus, like I mentioned, my father-in-law usually falls asleep in his recliner around 10 PM anyway. He'll just leave the TV blaring, but my wife will usually creep out to the living room and mute it or turn it way down once she hears him snoring. And she bought this white noise machine a couple of years ago—I used to absolutely hate that thing, I thought it sounded like a broken radio, but now? Now it is an absolute lifesaver. It just washes out whatever residual noise is coming down the hallway. \n\nSo, no, falling asleep isn't an issue for me at all. I sleep right through the night. And I'm not staying in bed all day or oversleeping, either. I still get up at my usual time, usually around 5:30 or 6:00 in the morning. I get up, I make my coffee, and I try to enjoy exactly thirty or forty minutes of absolute silence before I hear the click of the remote control in the living room and the morning news starts blasting at full volume. So my sleep schedule is exactly the same as it's always been. It's really just the hours I'm awake that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you finally have a quiet moment or some free time, you still struggle to get yourself to start doing the things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what happens. It's incredibly frustrating. Because, you know, my actual job—the EMT work—I’m still doing that just fine. When I'm on shift, I'm 100% there. Twenty years in the back of an ambulance, muscle memory just takes over. I show up, I do the job, I don't miss shifts. Work is almost like an escape at this point. \n\nBut everything else? It's exactly like you said. Even when I do get a window of time, I just stall out. Like that paramedic coursework I was telling you about earlier. I've completely fallen behind on it. It's ridiculous because I really do want the certification, and I know the material. But say my wife takes her dad to a doctor's appointment, right? The house is finally dead silent for maybe two hours. I'll sit down at the kitchen table, I'll open the laptop, pull up the modules... and I just stare at the screen. I'll read the same paragraph about cardiac pharmacology four times and retain absolutely none of it. I just can't get the engine to turn over. I end up just scrolling on my phone or staring out the window until they get back and the TV goes right back on. \n\nAnd it's not just the schoolwork, either. It's bleeding into other commitments. There are these community first-aid workshops I usually help out with at the rec center a few blocks over. We teach CPR, basic wound care, how to use an AED, that sort of thing. I've been doing it for, gosh, maybe five or six years now. Usually it's just once a month on a Saturday morning, and I used to really look forward to it. It's good for the neighborhood, it gets me out of the house. But lately? I've stopped showing up consistently. I'll text the coordinator the night before and make up an excuse, say I'm too tired from a shift, or I'll just conveniently \"forget\" to put it on my calendar so I double-book myself. \n\nI feel terribly guilty about it, I really do, because they rely on me to bring the training dummies and run the demonstrations. But the thought of going over there and being \"on\" and trying to teach a room full of twenty people when my own brain feels like it's wading through molasses... I just can't bring myself to do it. So yeah, I'm still clocking in for my actual paycheck, but all the other stuff I'm supposed to be doing is just slipping right through the cracks, even when I technically have the time to do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don't worry that I'm going to fail, like I said before. I know I can do the work. The only thing I guess you could say I \"worry\" about is just the logistics of it all. Like, how am I going to find three quiet hours to get this paper done before Sunday at midnight? But that's not, you know, irrational worrying. That's just a very real, practical problem caused by the fact that my living room has turned into a retirement home common area. \n\nSo it's not like my mind is spinning with all these hypothetical disasters. I'm not anxious about the future or anything like that. I'm just incredibly focused on the immediate problem, which is the noise and the disruption in my house. I mean, my father-in-law, he's a good guy, he really is, and I know it's hard for him right now, losing his independence and having to move in with his daughter and son-in-law. I get that. I try to be empathetic. But my God, the volume on that television. It's like a physical force. It rattles my teeth. And it's constant. From the minute he wakes up at 6 AM to the minute he finally falls asleep in his recliner at 10 PM. \n\nSo my brain is just entirely occupied by that. It's not worrying, it's just... hyper-awareness of the annoyance. I'll be at work, and instead of worrying about my next call, I'll just be dreading walking through my front door because I know exactly what it's going to sound like. But I don't sit around inventing things to be anxious about. The actual reality of my day-to-day life is stressful enough without making things up to worry about, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your sleep lately, like having trouble falling or staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, my sleep is actually... it's probably the one thing that hasn't been completely derailed by all of this. I mean, you would think, with all the noise and the frustration I was just telling you about, that I'd be tossing and turning all night, but honestly? Once I actually get into bed, I am out cold. \n\nI think a lot of that just comes down to my job. When you've been riding in an ambulance for over twenty years, you learn to sleep when you can, where you can. You train your body to just shut down the second your head hits a pillow, because you never know if you're going to get a call in ten minutes and be up for the next fourteen hours straight on a messy trauma. So I've got this, I don't know, this built-in ability to just crash from pure physical exhaustion. \n\nAnd, to be fair, our master bedroom is at the very back of the house. It's down the hall, past the kitchen, so the TV noise isn't quite as bad back there. Plus, like I mentioned, my father-in-law usually falls asleep in his recliner around 10 PM anyway. He'll just leave the TV blaring, but my wife will usually creep out to the living room and mute it or turn it way down once she hears him snoring. And she bought this white noise machine a couple of years ago—I used to absolutely hate that thing, I thought it sounded like a broken radio, but now? Now it is an absolute lifesaver. It just washes out whatever residual noise is coming down the hallway. \n\nSo, no, falling asleep isn't an issue for me at all. I sleep right through the night. And I'm not staying in bed all day or oversleeping, either. I still get up at my usual time, usually around 5:30 or 6:00 in the morning. I get up, I make my coffee, and I try to enjoy exactly thirty or forty minutes of absolute silence before I hear the click of the remote control in the living room and the morning news starts blasting at full volume. So my sleep schedule is exactly the same as it's always been. It's really just the hours I'm awake that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you finally have a quiet moment or some free time, you still struggle to get yourself to start doing the things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what happens. It's incredibly frustrating. Because, you know, my actual job—the EMT work—I’m still doing that just fine. When I'm on shift, I'm 100% there. Twenty years in the back of an ambulance, muscle memory just takes over. I show up, I do the job, I don't miss shifts. Work is almost like an escape at this point. \n\nBut everything else? It's exactly like you said. Even when I do get a window of time, I just stall out. Like that paramedic coursework I was telling you about earlier. I've completely fallen behind on it. It's ridiculous because I really do want the certification, and I know the material. But say my wife takes her dad to a doctor's appointment, right? The house is finally dead silent for maybe two hours. I'll sit down at the kitchen table, I'll open the laptop, pull up the modules... and I just stare at the screen. I'll read the same paragraph about cardiac pharmacology four times and retain absolutely none of it. I just can't get the engine to turn over. I end up just scrolling on my phone or staring out the window until they get back and the TV goes right back on. \n\nAnd it's not just the schoolwork, either. It's bleeding into other commitments. There are these community first-aid workshops I usually help out with at the rec center a few blocks over. We teach CPR, basic wound care, how to use an AED, that sort of thing. I've been doing it for, gosh, maybe five or six years now. Usually it's just once a month on a Saturday morning, and I used to really look forward to it. It's good for the neighborhood, it gets me out of the house. But lately? I've stopped showing up consistently. I'll text the coordinator the night before and make up an excuse, say I'm too tired from a shift, or I'll just conveniently \"forget\" to put it on my calendar so I double-book myself. \n\nI feel terribly guilty about it, I really do, because they rely on me to bring the training dummies and run the demonstrations. But the thought of going over there and being \"on\" and trying to teach a room full of twenty people when my own brain feels like it's wading through molasses... I just can't bring myself to do it. So yeah, I'm still clocking in for my actual paycheck, but all the other stuff I'm supposed to be doing is just slipping right through the cracks, even when I technically have the time to do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble thinking clearly. Not at all. I mean, my brain itself is working perfectly fine. Like I said, at work, I'm completely sharp. You can't be out there on the streets, making split-second decisions about airway management or, you know, pushing the right meds during a cardiac event, if you're dealing with brain fog. I'm calculating dosages in my head in the back of a moving ambulance doing sixty miles an hour with sirens blaring, and I haven't made a single mistake. So my thinking is as clear as it's ever been. 

And even at home, it's not a focus issue in the sense that my brain is broken or I can't concentrate on anything. If I'm reading the sports page, or if I'm looking over our household budget and paying the bills online, I can focus on that just fine. I track everything perfectly. So when I mentioned earlier about staring at that pharmacology paragraph and not retaining it... that's not because I'm losing my ability to concentrate. It's more like... it's just a total refusal to engage. It's like a stubborn mule just planting its hooves in the dirt. I'm just so drained by the constant noise and the tension in the house that when I finally sit down to do something demanding for myself, the motivation just isn't there to process it. 

But if you're asking if I'm getting confused, or if my thoughts are jumbled or cloudy in general? No, absolutely not. I'm completely lucid. My memory is fine, my problem-solving is fine. I can follow complex conversations, I can watch a movie and follow the plot perfectly—well, assuming I could actually hear the dialogue over my father-in-law's television, which is a big 'if' these days. But cognitively speaking? No, my focus and my actual ability to think are exactly the same as they've always been. It's strictly a mental block with the extra work because I'm just so worn down by my living room being turned into a movie theater for the hearing impaired, you know? But my mind itself is 100% intact.

Like, I love fishing. I usually try to get out to the water at least a couple times a month, just to decompress and get away from the noise. And I have a whole woodworking setup in the garage that I haven't even looked at in weeks. I've even started skipping out on these community first-aid workshops I usually help run on my weekends off. I just... I don't have the bandwidth for any of it right now, and sitting around the house all wound up is just making it worse, so I figured I better come talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not getting the same enjoyment out of things you used to really like, like fishing or woodworking?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the distinction I guess I need to make. It's not that I don't think I'd enjoy them if I was actually doing them. I mean, I've been fishing since I was a little kid—my old man used to take me out to this little spot by the dam every Sunday, rain or shine—so that love for the water, it's baked into me. I know I'd still like it. And the woodworking, too. I've actually got this really beautiful piece of cherry wood just sitting out there on the workbench right now. I was in the middle of planning out a new coffee table for the living room, something with a nice live edge, you know? I had the whole design sketched out, the epoxy bought, the sander ready to go. \n\nBut it's not a lack of enjoyment, it's more like... I don't know, a lack of activation energy? I just look at the door to the garage, and I think about going out there and setting up, and I just can't muster the drive to actually turn the doorknob. Because my brain is just buzzing so loud with everything else—the coursework deadlines, the constant shuffling around my father-in-law. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. I just haven't even tried to do them lately to find out. Getting started just feels like climbing a mountain right now, so I just end up sitting on the couch instead, feeling my chest get tighter.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t care about doing things you used to enjoy, even when you have the chance?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's not that I don't care. I care a lot, actually. That's kind of what's so frustrating about this whole situation. I mean, I sit there on the couch, and I can literally see my tackle box sitting by the back door where I left it after my last trip, which was... gosh, it must have been early last month before my father-in-law's lease was up and we had to move him in. And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I want to be doing those things. I miss doing those things. It's just... like I was saying before, it's the getting up and actually doing it part that feels impossible right now. It's like my battery is just completely drained by the time I have a free moment. I'll have a whole Sunday afternoon wide open, the weather will be perfect, sixty-five degrees and partly cloudy, ideal for getting out on the water, and I just... I can't. I just sit there feeling wound up and tense, wishing I was fishing, but not having the energy to actually put the gear in the truck and drive to the lake. So, yeah, I still care. Very much so. It just doesn't seem to translate into action right now.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had days where you felt down or lost interest in things for most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not... not exactly. I wouldn't say I feel *down*, like, you know, sad or depressed or anything like that. It's not a sadness. It's more of this... this constant, low-level hum of tension, like I mentioned with the tight chest. It's like I'm a coiled spring, just waiting for the next thing to snap. \n\nAnd as for losing interest, well, I think we kind of covered that, right? I haven't lost interest. I still *want* to be out in the garage sanding that cherry wood, or out on the lake with my rod and reel. I really do. The interest is 100% there. It's just the execution that's failing me. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I've had days where I'm just sitting around feeling down and not caring about anything. It's almost the opposite. I care too much, I'm wound too tight, and my brain is just so overloaded with the coursework and the new living situation with my father-in-law that I just... I freeze up. I just sit there on the couch, tense as can be, thinking about all the things I *should* be doing, or *want* to be doing, and just not doing them. But it's definitely not a feeling of being down or sad. Just... overwhelmed, I guess. Overwhelmed and exhausted.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself getting more irritable or angry lately, even over small things?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess that’s exactly it. That’s really the whole reason I made the appointment to come in. It’s like, at work—and being an EMT, you know work is high stakes, it’s literally life or death sometimes—I can keep it together. I’m focused, I’m professional, I’m doing my job. But the second I clock out and come home... it’s a different story. \n\nEver since my father-in-law moved in with us, I’ve just been so wound up. Like I said, he’s got the TV blasting, he’s always shuffling around, and I’m just... I’m definitely shorter with people than I’m used to being. It doesn't take much to set me off. And it’s bleeding into my coursework, too. I just sit there staring at the screen, feeling my jaw clench, getting frustrated over the smallest little assignment. \n\nAnd because I’m so on edge, I’ve started skipping all the things that usually keep my head clear. I’m not fishing, I’m not touching the woodworking projects, I even stopped going to these first-aid workshops I usually help run on the weekends. I used to love doing those, sharing some of my field experience, you know? But now, I just can't. I’m just so tightly wound all the time at home, snapping at little things, that I just shut down and avoid everything that usually helps me decompress.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt unusually tired or noticed a big drop in your energy levels, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. \n\nIt's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? I just haven't been able to actually engage with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself worrying a lot about different things—like work, school, or home life—even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. I don't lay in bed stressing about whether I missed a vein on an IV or if I should have done something differently on a call. I leave it at the station. \n\nAnd with school... I don't worry that I'm going to fail, like I said before. I know I can do the work. The only thing I guess you could say I \"worry\" about is just the logistics of it all. I'm not anxious about the future or anything like that. I'm just incredibly focused on the immediate problem, which is the noise and the disruption in my house. He'll just leave the TV blaring, but my wife will usually creep out to the living room and mute it or turn it way down once she hears him snoring. I get up, I make my coffee, and I try to enjoy exactly thirty or forty minutes of absolute silence before I hear the click of the remote control in the living room and the morning news starts blasting at full volume. So my sleep schedule is exactly the same as it's always been. It's really just the hours I'm awake that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you finally have a quiet moment or some free time, you still struggle to get yourself to start doing the things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what happens. It's incredibly frustrating. Because, you know, my actual job—the EMT work—I’m still doing that just fine. When I'm on shift, I'm 100% there. Twenty years in the back of an ambulance, muscle memory just takes over. I show up, I do the job, I don't miss shifts. Work is almost like an escape at this point. \n\nBut everything else? It's exactly like you said. Even when I do get a window of time, I just stall out. Like that paramedic coursework I was telling you about earlier. I've completely fallen behind on it. It's ridiculous because I really do want the certification, and I know the material. But say my wife takes her dad to a doctor's appointment, right? The house is finally dead silent for maybe two hours. I'll sit down at the kitchen table, I'll open the laptop, pull up the modules... and I just stare at the screen. I'll read the same paragraph about cardiac pharmacology four times and retain absolutely none of it. I just can't get the engine to turn over. I end up just scrolling on my phone or staring out the window until they get back and the TV goes right back on. \n\nAnd it's not just the schoolwork, either. It's bleeding into other commitments. There are these community first-aid workshops I usually help out with at the rec center a few blocks over. We teach CPR, basic wound care, how to use an AED, that sort of thing. I've been doing it for, gosh, maybe five or six years now. Usually it's just once a month on a Saturday morning, and I used to really look forward to it. It's good for the neighborhood, it gets me out of the house. But lately? I've stopped showing up consistently. I'll text the coordinator the night before and make up an excuse, say I'm too tired from a shift, or I'll just conveniently \"forget\" to put it on my calendar so I double-book myself. \n\nI feel terribly guilty about it, I really do, because they rely on me to bring the training dummies and run the demonstrations. But the thought of going over there and being \"on\" and trying to teach a room full of twenty people when my own brain feels like it's wading through molasses... I just can't bring myself to do it. So yeah, I'm still clocking in for my actual paycheck, but all the other stuff I'm supposed to be doing is just slipping right through the cracks, even when I technically have the time to do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or thinking clearly lately, especially when you're trying to work on your coursework or read?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble thinking clearly. Not at all. I mean, my brain itself is working perfectly fine. Like I said, at work, I'm completely sharp. You can't be out there on the streets, making split-second decisions about airway management or, you know, pushing the right meds during a cardiac event, if you're dealing with brain fog. I'm calculating dosages in my head in the back of a moving ambulance doing sixty miles an hour with sirens blaring, and I haven't made a single mistake. If I'm reading the sports page, or if I'm looking over our household budget and paying the bills online, I can focus on that just fine. I track everything perfectly. But cognitively speaking? No, my focus and my actual ability to think are exactly the same as they've always been. It's strictly a mental block with the extra work because I'm just so worn down by my living room being turned into a movie theater for the hearing impaired, you know? But my mind itself is 100% intact.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I used to love doing those, sharing some of my field experience, you know? But now, I just can't. I’m just so tightly wound all the time at home, snapping at little things, that I just shut down and avoid everything that usually helps me decompress.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt unusually tired or noticed a big drop in your energy levels, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. I mean, I don't feel like I'm a failure or that I'm not good enough, if that's what you're asking. It's not a self-esteem issue. Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been an EMT for a long time, and I know I can handle the pressure out there in the field. When I'm on a call, I know exactly what I'm doing, and I don't second-guess myself. And even with the coursework, I know I'm smart enough to do it. I've always been a decent student when I put my mind to it. \n\nIt's really just the situation at home that's throwing me off. It's not that I feel bad about *myself*, it's that I feel bad about how I'm reacting to *him*. You know? Like, I'll snap at my wife because the TV is too loud, and then I'll feel guilty about that, because it's not her fault her dad needs to live with us right now. But it's not this deep, dark feeling of worthlessness. It's just... frustration. Frustration that I can't seem to just put on a pair of noise-canceling headphones and power through my reading like a normal person. \n\nBut I don't sit around thinking I'm a terrible person or a failure. I just think I'm a guy who is incredibly stressed out by a major change in his living situation and hasn't figured out how to adapt to it yet. I mean, anyone would be stressed if their quiet sanctuary suddenly turned into a 24/7 game show broadcasting network, right? So, no, I wouldn't say I'm feeling worse about myself in that way. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even when you have the chance to do them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a loss of interest. I still *want* to do the things I enjoy. Like, I was saying earlier about my garage—I have this whole setup out there, a workbench where I do some woodworking, restore old furniture, that kind of thing. It's usually my way to decompress after a rough shift. And I still really, really want to be out there. I'll be sitting on the couch, hearing the TV blaring, and I'll be thinking about this oak coffee table I started sanding down three months ago, right before he moved in. I can picture exactly what I need to do next, which grit of sandpaper to use, how I want the stain to look... \n\nSo the interest is absolutely still there. It's not like I look at my tools and feel nothing, or like I don't care about my hobbies anymore. It's just that I can't seem to get myself out there to actually *do* it. Because by the time I have a free hour, I'm just so mentally drained from the noise and the tension in the house that I just sit there. It's like I'm paralyzed by the environment. \n\nAnd it's the same with my coursework. I'm actually really interested in the material, it's for an advanced certification I've wanted to get for years. I *want* to read the textbook, I *want* to write the papers. But I just can't focus. So, if I had the chance—like, if the house was completely empty and quiet for a whole weekend—I know for a fact I'd be out in the garage in a heartbeat, or happily buried in my books. The desire hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... the execution is blocked by all this stress and noise. So no, I haven't lost interest or pleasure in those things. I just haven't been able to actually engage with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself worrying a lot about different things—like work, school, or home life—even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. I mean, I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around chewing my fingernails, thinking, \"Oh no, what if this happens, what if that happens?\" Like, with work, I don't worry about work when I'm not there. I've been doing this for over twenty years. When I clock out, I clock out. I don't lay in bed stressing about whether I missed a vein on an IV or if I should have done something differently on a call. I leave it at the station. \n\nAnd with school... I don't worry that I'm going to fail, like I said before. I know I can do the work. The only thing I guess you could say I \"worry\" about is just the logistics of it all. Like, how am I going to find three quiet hours to get this paper done before Sunday at midnight? But that's not, you know, irrational worrying. That's just a very real, practical problem caused by the fact that my living room has turned into a retirement home common area. \n\nSo it's not like my mind is spinning with all these hypothetical disasters. I'm not anxious about the future or anything like that. I'm just incredibly focused on the immediate problem, which is the noise and the disruption in my house. I mean, my father-in-law, he's a good guy, he really is, and I know it's hard for him right now, losing his independence and having to move in with his daughter and son-in-law. I get that. I try to be empathetic. But my God, the volume on that television. It's like a physical force. It rattles my teeth. And it's constant. From the minute he wakes up at 6 AM to the minute he finally falls asleep in his recliner at 10 PM. \n\nSo my brain is just entirely occupied by that. It's not worrying, it's just... hyper-awareness of the annoyance. I'll be at work, and instead of worrying about my next call, I'll just be dreading walking through my front door because I know exactly what it's going to sound like. But I don't sit around inventing things to be anxious about. The actual reality of my day-to-day life is stressful enough without making things up to worry about, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your sleep lately, like having trouble falling or staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, no, my sleep is actually... it's probably the one thing that hasn't been completely derailed by all of this. I mean, you would think, with all the noise and the frustration I was just telling you about, that I'd be tossing and turning all night, but honestly? Once I actually get into bed, I am out cold. \n\nI think a lot of that just comes down to my job. When you've been riding in an ambulance for over twenty years, you learn to sleep when you can, where you can. You train your body to just shut down the second your head hits a pillow, because you never know if you're going to get a call in ten minutes and be up for the next fourteen hours straight on a messy trauma. So I've got this, I don't know, this built-in ability to just crash from pure physical exhaustion. \n\nAnd, to be fair, our master bedroom is at the very back of the house. It's down the hall, past the kitchen, so the TV noise isn't quite as bad back there. Plus, like I mentioned, my father-in-law usually falls asleep in his recliner around 10 PM anyway. He'll just leave the TV blaring, but my wife will usually creep out to the living room and mute it or turn it way down once she hears him snoring. And she bought this white noise machine a couple of years ago—I used to absolutely hate that thing, I thought it sounded like a broken radio, but now? Now it is an absolute lifesaver. It just washes out whatever residual noise is coming down the hallway. \n\nSo, no, falling asleep isn't an issue for me at all. I sleep right through the night. And I'm not staying in bed all day or oversleeping, either. I still get up at my usual time, usually around 5:30 or 6:00 in the morning. I get up, I make my coffee, and I try to enjoy exactly thirty or forty minutes of absolute silence before I hear the click of the remote control in the living room and the morning news starts blasting at full volume. So my sleep schedule is exactly the same as it's always been. It's really just the hours I'm awake that are the problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that even when you finally have a quiet moment or some free time, you still struggle to get yourself to start doing the things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that's exactly what happens. It's incredibly frustrating. Because, you know, my actual job—the EMT work—I’m still doing that just fine. When I'm on shift, I'm 100% there. Twenty years in the back of an ambulance, muscle memory just takes over. I show up, I do the job, I don't miss shifts. Work is almost like an escape at this point. \n\nBut everything else? It's exactly like you said. Even when I do get a window of time, I just stall out. Like that paramedic coursework I was telling you about earlier. I've completely fallen behind on it. It's ridiculous because I really do want the certification, and I know the material. But say my wife takes her dad to a doctor's appointment, right? The house is finally dead silent for maybe two hours. I'll sit down at the kitchen table, I'll open the laptop, pull up the modules... and I just stare at the screen. I'll read the same paragraph about cardiac pharmacology four times and retain absolutely none of it. I just can't get the engine to turn over. I end up just scrolling on my phone or staring out the window until they get back and the TV goes right back on. \n\nAnd it's not just the schoolwork, either. It's bleeding into other commitments. There are these community first-aid workshops I usually help out with at the rec center a few blocks over. We teach CPR, basic wound care, how to use an AED, that sort of thing. I've been doing it for, gosh, maybe five or six years now. Usually it's just once a month on a Saturday morning, and I used to really look forward to it. It's good for the neighborhood, it gets me out of the house. But lately? I've stopped showing up consistently. I'll text the coordinator the night before and make up an excuse, say I'm too tired from a shift, or I'll just conveniently \"forget\" to put it on my calendar so I double-book myself. \n\nI feel terribly guilty about it, I really do, because they rely on me to bring the training dummies and run the demonstrations. But the thought of going over there and being \"on\" and trying to teach a room full of twenty people when my own brain feels like it's wading through molasses... I just can't bring myself to do it. So yeah, I'm still clocking in for my actual paycheck, but all the other stuff I'm supposed to be doing is just slipping right through the cracks, even when I technically have the time to do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or thinking clearly lately, especially when you're trying to work on your coursework or read?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble thinking clearly. Not at all. I mean, my brain itself is working perfectly fine. Like I said, at work, I'm completely sharp. You can't be out there on the streets, making split-second decisions about airway management or, you know, pushing the right meds during a cardiac event, if you're dealing with brain fog. I'm calculating dosages in my head in the back of a moving ambulance doing sixty miles an hour with sirens blaring, and I haven't made a single mistake. So my thinking is as clear as it's ever been. \n\nAnd even at home, it's not a focus issue in the sense that my brain is broken or I can't concentrate on anything. If I'm reading the sports page, or if I'm looking over our household budget and paying the bills online, I can focus on that just fine. I track everything perfectly. So when I mentioned earlier about staring at that pharmacology paragraph and not retaining it... that's not because I'm losing my ability to concentrate. It's more like... it's just a total refusal to engage. It's like a stubborn mule just planting its hooves in the dirt. I'm just so drained by the constant noise and the tension in the house that when I finally sit down to do something demanding for myself, the motivation just isn't there to process it. \n\nBut if you're asking if I'm getting confused, or if my thoughts are jumbled or cloudy in general? No, absolutely not. I'm completely lucid. My memory is fine, my problem-solving is fine. I can follow complex conversations, I can watch a movie and follow the plot perfectly—well, assuming I could actually hear the dialogue over my father-in-law's television, which is a big 'if' these days. But cognitively speaking? No, my focus and my actual ability to think are exactly the same as they've always been. It's strictly a mental block with the extra work because I'm just so worn down by my living room being turned into a movie theater for the hearing impaired, you know? But my mind itself is 100% intact.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, that's not it at all. When I actually get to do those things, the enjoyment is absolutely still there. Take woodworking, for example, since you mentioned it. I have a little setup in our detached garage out back. It's nothing fancy, I mean, just a sturdy workbench I built myself about fifteen years ago, an old Delta table saw I picked up at an estate sale, a decent router... but it is my space. And when I'm out there, I love it just as much as I always have. Honestly, maybe even more right now, because it's the one place on the entire property where I can't hear that television blasting from the living room. 

Just the smell of the sawdust, the hum of the sander, the tactile feel of the grain... it's incredibly grounding for me. Last Sunday, I actually managed to get out there for maybe three hours to work on this custom oak shelving unit for our pantry. My wife has been asking for it since last Thanksgiving, and I finally got around to routing the edges and doing the initial sanding, and I felt fantastic. I was completely in the zone. It felt incredibly rewarding to see the joints fit perfectly together. 

So the problem isn't that I don't enjoy it. I absolutely do. The joy of the hobby itself hasn't faded one bit. I still get a huge sense of satisfaction from working with my hands and building something tangible, especially after a long shift of dealing with medical emergencies where things are so chaotic and unpredictable. Wood makes sense, you know? You measure twice, you cut once, it fits. It's logical. 

It's really just that getting the uninterrupted time to actually do it is so rare these days. Because I'll be out there, finally getting into a groove, lining up a tricky cut, and then the garage side door opens and it's my wife telling me her dad needs help getting out of his recliner, or the pharmacy called about his heart medication and someone needs to run into town. The real world just constantly intrudes. But as far as the actual pleasure of the activity? No, that hasn't changed at all. If anything, it's a lifeline. I just wish I had more quiet hours to actually experience it.

But the thought of going over there and being \"on\" and trying to teach a room full of twenty people when my own brain feels like it's wading through molasses... I just can't bring myself to do it. So yeah, I'm still clocking in for my actual paycheck, but all the other stuff I'm supposed to be doing is just slipping right through the cracks, even when I technically have the time to do it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble focusing or thinking clearly lately, especially when you're trying to work on your coursework or read?\n\nPatient: No, no, I wouldn't say I have trouble thinking clearly. Wait, didn't I... I feel like we might be crossing wires here, doc, because like I was just saying, no, not at all. The satisfaction is absolutely still there. 

Like I mentioned with the woodworking and the garage, I still get a huge amount of pleasure out of it. And it's the exact same story with fishing, since you brought that one up too. If I actually manage to get my gear loaded into the back of the truck and drive down to the reservoir—which, let me tell you, is a whole production these days, just trying to slip out without feeling this massive wave of guilt for leaving my wife alone to deal with her dad and the constant noise—but if I *do* manage to get out there, it's fantastic. It feels just as good as it did ten or twenty years ago. 

I'll sit out there on the bank with my line in the water, and just the quiet... oh, the quiet out there is incredible. Just hearing the water lap against the rocks instead of hearing game shows at maximum volume. And feeling that tug on the line, reeling in a decent-sized bass, it's still the best feeling in the world to me. So, no, the actual joy of doing those things hasn't faded even a fraction of a percent. 

It's not like the food has lost its taste, you know what I mean? It's just that I can't ever seem to get a seat at the table to actually eat it. When I'm actively engaged in the hobby, whether it's sanding down that oak pantry shelf I was telling you about or casting a line out on the water, my mind is fully engaged, I'm happy, I feel completely like my old self. It's strictly the surrounding circumstances at the house that are dragging me down, not the activities themselves. Honestly, if I could just get a solid weekend away at a cabin somewhere with no cell service and no one needing help out of a recliner, I'd be fishing from sunup to sundown and I'd be loving every single second of it.

I'm holding it together perfectly fine at work. Actually, he’s got his recliner set up right near the hallway that leads to the garage, and he watches his historical documentaries at absolute top volume because his hearing isn't what it used to be, so even just walking past him to get to my tools feels like an obstacle course I don't want to run. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed a drop in how much I *like* those hobbies. And I look at that tackle box, and I think about how nice it would be to just be out on the lake, you know, feeling the breeze, listening to the water lap against the side of the boat, not having to worry about my coursework or whether the TV is going to be blaring at level fifty when I walk into the living room. \n\nSo the desire is there, the caring is definitely there. I’m just so tightly wound all the time at home, snapping at little things, that I just shut down and avoid everything that usually helps me decompress.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt unusually tired or noticed a big drop in your energy levels, even when you’re not doing anything physically or mentally demanding?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, I know I said earlier that I feel exhausted, like my battery is completely drained by the time I have a free moment, but it's not like a physical tiredness, if that makes sense. It's not like I'm dragging myself out of bed in the morning or feeling like I need to take a nap in the middle of the day. As an EMT, you know, I work some pretty crazy shifts, and I'm used to being on my feet and running on adrenaline. So physically, my energy is fine. I can still lift a stretcher, I can still hustle when a call comes in. \n\nIt's more of a mental exhaustion. Like, when I'm at work, I'm totally dialed in. But when I come home and I'm faced with the TV blaring and my coursework staring me in the face, that's when I just hit a wall. But it's not a lack of energy, per se. It's more like... I'm just so tense and wound up from dealing with the situation at home that I don't have the mental bandwidth to do anything else. So I wouldn't say I've noticed a big drop in my energy levels overall, or that I feel unusually tired in the sense of needing sleep. It's just that all my energy is being eaten up by this constant state of frustration and tension, leaving me with nothing left in the tank for the things I actually want to do. So, no, not really tired in the way you're asking, just... mentally depleted, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the contrary, have you been more restless or fidgety lately?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not moving slowly. Definitely not that. I mean, like I said, at work I'm moving at a million miles an hour. You have to in this job, right? You can't be sluggish when you're pulling up to a multi-car pileup or trying to stabilize someone having a heart attack. You have to be sharp, you have to be quick. So, no, my movements aren't slow, and I'm definitely not talking slower either. If anything, I probably talk faster when I'm stressed out, just trying to get everything out of my head. \n\nBut as for being restless or fidgety... I mean, I wouldn't say I'm pacing the floors or anything like that. It's more of an internal restlessness, you know? Like I was saying before about being a coiled spring. It's not like I'm constantly tapping my foot or bouncing my leg—well, maybe a little bit when I'm sitting on the couch trying to ignore the television while my father-in-law watches his game shows at maximum volume. But it's not this uncontrollable physical fidgeting. \n\nIt's more just that I can't settle down. I sit there, and my mind is racing with all the things I need to do—the reading for my courses, the papers I need to write, the fact that I haven't been out to the garage in weeks. But my body just kind of stays put, tense, but not necessarily moving around a lot. So, no, I wouldn't say I'm noticeably slower or noticeably more fidgety than usual. Just... stuck. Stuck in this weird, tense holding pattern where my brain is going a mile a minute but I'm just sitting there, completely paralyzed by the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling worse about yourself lately, like you're not good enough or that you're failing at things you should be able to handle?\n\nPatient: Well, no... not really. Just... annoyed at my own inability to tune it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in activities you usually enjoy, even when you have the chance to do them?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not a loss of interest. I'll be sitting on the couch, hearing the TV blaring, and I'll be thinking about this oak coffee table I started sanding down three months ago, right before he moved in. The desire hasn't gone anywhere at all. It's just... the execution is blocked by all this stress and noise. So no, I haven't lost interest or pleasure in those things. I just haven't been able to actually engage with them lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself worrying a lot about different things—like work, school, or home life—even when there isn’t a clear reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really worrying. I mean, I wouldn't call it worrying, exactly. It's not like I'm sitting around chewing my fingernails, thinking, \"Oh no, what if this happens, what if that happens?\" Like, with work, I don't worry about work when I'm not there. I've been doing this for over twenty years. When I clock out, I clock out. I don't lay in bed stressing about whether I missed a vein on an IV or if I should have done something differently on a call. I leave it at the station. \n\nAnd with school...
